I’m a business operations specialist, and I have my degree and everything, so my whole job is literally making sure things run efficiently and fixing problems. But lately, if even one minor thing gets delayed or a spreadsheet is slightly off, I just spiral completely. I sit at my desk and just think about what a bad employee I am, how I'm probably ruining everything for my team and I should just be fired. \n\nAnd it doesn't stop when I clock out, either. I go home, to this house I bought, and instead of feeling good about it, I just look at the dust or the yard or a leaky faucet and I feel like a completely bad homeowner. Like I have no business being responsible for a property. I just... I look at my life and I honestly feel like I deserve to lose everything I’ve worked so hard for. Like I’m a fraud who doesn't deserve the house or the job, and it’s all my fault that things aren't perfect. I just keep ruminating on how guilty I feel about every little mistake, and I can't turn it off, no matter how much I try to rationalize it. It's just exhausting feeling like everything is ruined because of me.\n\nDoctor: Do these guilt thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Um... intrusive. I don't... I'm not really sure if I would use that word? Because when you say they pop into my mind, it kind of makes it sound like my mind is ever clear to begin with, you know? Like I'm just sitting there having a perfectly fine Tuesday, drinking my coffee, and then suddenly a random bad thought just attacks me out of nowhere. But it's... it’s not really like that. It’s not a pop-in. It’s just sort of always there. It’s the baseline. \n\nLike, I wake up in the morning and that heavy feeling is already sitting on my chest before I even open my eyes or look at my work phone to check on the overnight reports. And it's not like I feel like the thoughts are these foreign, weird invaders that I'm trying to push away. I mean, I do want to feel better, obviously, because feeling this way is just completely draining my energy and making it so hard to function, but the thoughts themselves just feel like... well, like they're just the truth. Like I'm just observing the reality of how badly I'm doing at everything and how much I'm messing up. \n\nSo it’s less of a sudden, unwanted pop-in and more of just a continuous, running commentary that I can’t escape. I was actually trying to explain this to my sister the other day—she doesn't really get the pressure of my job at all, but I was trying to tell her that it's just this constant loop. It doesn't pop in, it just never really leaves. It's just always playing in the background, like really terrible elevator music that just reminds me of every single thing I've failed at, whether it's a delayed project or just the dust on my baseboards. So, yeah, I wouldn't really say they pop in, because they're already just... there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um... depressed? I mean... I don't really know if I would call it that? I'm honestly not sure. Because when I hear the word depressed, I usually think of someone who just lies in bed all day in the dark, crying and not doing anything, you know? Like they just completely give up. And that's not really me. I mean, I still get up every single morning at 6:00 AM, I make my coffee, I log onto my computer for work—because as an operations specialist, if I'm not online checking the supply chain metrics by 7:30, things actually will fall apart, and then it really would be my fault. So I'm functioning, I'm doing things. I even went to the hardware store this weekend to get a new filter for my HVAC system because, you know, homeownership. \n\nSo I don't know if I feel \"down\" exactly. It's more just... like I was saying before, it's just this constant, heavy guilt and this feeling that I'm messing everything up. I'm just so focused on how bad of a job I'm doing at literally everything that I don't even know if I have time to feel depressed, if that makes sense? I'm just constantly scanning my environment, whether it's my email inbox or my living room, looking for the next thing I've ruined. \n\nLike yesterday, I spent two hours agonizing over an email I sent to a vendor because I thought my tone was too abrupt and that I probably ruined the entire vendor relationship, which would impact our Q3 deliverables. I felt terrible, just completely sick over it, but... is that being depressed? I really haven't noticed feeling sad or down in the way people usually mean it. I just feel like a bad person who is failing at life. So, I guess I'm not really sure what you mean by depressed in this context. Does it count if I'm still getting all my work done, just feeling incredibly guilty while I do it?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad, empty, or hopeless most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Um... sad, empty, or hopeless. Empty is definitely the wrong word. I mean, honestly, I almost wish I felt empty sometimes? Because empty sounds quiet. Empty sounds like there’s nothing there, like a blank slate. But my head is just... it's so loud and crowded all the time. It’s like a web browser with a hundred tabs open, and every single tab is a different mistake I’ve made or a different way I'm letting people down. \n\nLike, just the other day, I was auditing our quarterly expense reports—which is usually my favorite part of the month because I actually really like catching the little discrepancies and fixing them—but instead of just doing my job, my chest just felt so incredibly heavy. I kept thinking about how I probably missed something in the previous quarter and how the finance team is going to find out and realize I'm a total fraud who doesn't deserve her degree. So I'm definitely not empty. I am filled to the absolute brim with dread and guilt. There's no room to be empty because I'm constantly cataloging every single flaw in my performance.\n\nAnd as for sad or hopeless... I really don't think so? Like I was saying about the word depressed, I'm not sitting around weeping. I don't feel hopeless about the world or the future of the company or anything like that. The company actually has a really great Q4 projection, and the housing market in my neighborhood is appreciating, so there's plenty of hope for *things*. I just feel like *I* am the problem in the middle of all those things. \n\nI haven't given up hope that things can run smoothly, I just think I'm the one ruining them. So I'm still trying. I'm still sending the emails, I'm still updating the workflow trackers, I still water the hydrangeas in my front yard even though I'm convinced I'm pruning them wrong and destroying the curb appeal. I'm putting in all this effort, so I don't feel hopeless. I just feel this crushing, continuous weight that I'm doing it all wrong and that it's all my fault. So, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or hopeless, and definitely, definitely not empty. It just takes up way too much space in my brain to ever feel empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, not just mistakes at work or around the house?\n\nPatient: Um... a lot of different things? No, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I'm definitely not a general worrywart. My sister is kind of like that, actually. She watches the news and gets completely spun out about macroeconomic trends, or she worries about whether her kids are going to catch whatever bug is going around at daycare, or she frets about getting a flat tire on the highway. I really don't do any of that. \n\nMy brain honestly just doesn't have the bandwidth to worry about random external things, because it is entirely consumed by what I am doing wrong. So if you mean worrying about things outside of my control, like the weather or traffic or other people's problems, no, I don't really care about any of that. I only agonize over things where I am the variable that ruins everything. \n\nLike, if a freight shipment is delayed because of a massive storm in the Pacific, I don't worry about the storm itself. The storm is just a data point in my logistics software. But I will absolutely spend twelve straight hours agonizing over the fact that I should have somehow anticipated that storm and rerouted the shipment through a different port, and how the delay is a direct reflection of my personal incompetence, and how I have completely let the entire supply chain team down. \n\nSo it is never a broad, generalized worry about a bunch of different topics. It is extremely specific. It is just a very targeted, relentless focus on my own failures and the ways I am messing up my job or my house. If a situation doesn't involve me making a mistake or being a terrible employee or homeowner, it just doesn't even register on my radar. So, no, I wouldn't say I worry about a lot of different things at all.\n\nDoctor: Has this harsh self-criticism been part of how you’ve been for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Um... most of my adult life? No, definitely not. I mean, back in college and even during my first couple of years working as a junior analyst, I didn't feel this way at all. I was actually pretty confident in my work back then. I knew I was doing a good job, and if I made a minor typo on a supply chain spreadsheet, I just fixed it and moved on. I didn't spend twelve hours agonizing over it and assuming I was going to bankrupt the company. \n\nThis really started... well, I know exactly when it started. It's been going on for about two years and three months. I know the exact timeline because that was right after I bought my townhouse. Which, you know, is supposed to be this huge, exciting milestone, especially being able to do it in my mid-twenties. But almost the second I signed the closing papers and got the keys, this immense pressure just started to build. Because a house isn't just a place to live, it's a massive financial asset, and keeping it up is entirely on my shoulders. \n\nSuddenly I was entirely responsible for the property taxes, the HOA compliance, the roof, the plumbing, the structural integrity... and if any of it went wrong, it would be a catastrophic failure on my part. I started feeling this intense, suffocating pressure to keep everything perfect so the property value wouldn't drop. Every time the HVAC made a weird noise or I saw a dandelion in the front mulch, I felt like I was completely failing at this huge responsibility. And then that feeling just sort of... bled over into my job and everything else. \n\nIt was like a switch flipped exactly two years and three months ago where suddenly every single action I took had these massive, high-stakes consequences, and I was convinced I was ruining all of them. So before I bought the place, no, I wasn't like this. But ever since that closing date, the pressure of keeping the townhouse just compounded and compounded, and that constant, harsh criticizing voice became my entire baseline.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after buying your townhouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it’s been about two years and three months. I know that sounds weirdly specific to keep repeating, but when you buy real estate, the dates are just completely burned into your brain, you know? You have the inspection date, the closing date, the day the first mortgage payment is due... and it was right around then. Right after I actually got the keys and moved my boxes in, that's when it all just started. \n\nBecause suddenly it wasn't just a fun Zillow listing anymore. It was real. The pressure of keeping it up just began to build almost immediately. I remember sitting in the living room on the floor—because my sofa hadn't been delivered yet, there was a whole logistics issue with the freight carrier, which of course I spent hours analyzing because that's what I do for a living—but I was sitting on the floor just looking at the baseboards and realizing that if there was termite damage, or if the foundation settled, or if I didn't maintain the HVAC filters properly, it was one hundred percent my fault. I was solely responsible for protecting this massive financial asset. \n\nAnd that pressure, that absolute crushing weight of knowing I had to keep everything perfect so the property value wouldn't tank... it just started building and building from that exact point two years and three months ago. It just snowballed from the house into my job and into every single thing I do. So, yeah, that was definitely the catalyst.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings caused clear problems in your life, like at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, they definitely have. It's... it's actually become a pretty significant issue across the board, honestly. I mean, starting with my social life, which feels almost nonexistent right now. I've missed so many plans with friends because I just get entirely bogged down in my own head. Like, a friend will text me to meet up for coffee or grab dinner, and instead of just saying yes, I start spiraling about how I don't deserve to go out because I haven't finished organizing the utility closet, or because I'm just going to bring everyone down with my incompetence. So I just isolate myself. I cancel at the last minute, or I just don't reply until it's too late, and then I feel even worse about being a terrible friend. \n\nAnd then at home... well, the townhouse, which is the whole source of this pressure to begin with, is just... it's getting messier than I can stand. Which is so deeply ironic, right? Because I'm so obsessed with keeping it perfect and maintaining the property value, but the actual day-to-day upkeep is just slipping through my fingers. I'll look at a pile of mail on the counter or a dusty baseboard, and instead of just cleaning it, I get completely paralyzed by the fact that I let it get that way in the first place. I just stare at it and mentally berate myself for being such a failure of a homeowner, to the point where I literally can't summon the energy to pick up a sponge. So the mess just compounds, and the guilt compounds with it.\n\nBut the worst part, the part that's really terrifying me, is work. Because logistics and operations, it's all about precision. You have to be on top of a million moving parts. And I've been making mistakes. Not huge, catastrophic ones yet, but enough little slip-ups—missing an email about a freight embargo, or miscalculating a warehouse turnaround time—that my supervisor actually pulled me aside last week. She started checking in on my performance, asking if my workload was too heavy or if I needed additional support. Which, of course, just sent me into an absolute tailspin, because having your boss ask if you're struggling is basically confirmation that you are failing at your job. \n\nI'm still working, I haven't taken a leave of absence or anything, but it takes so much more effort just to keep up with my basic responsibilities. I have to triple-check every single spreadsheet cell because I don't trust my own brain anymore. What used to take me an hour now takes me three, just because I'm constantly second-guessing myself and fighting through this thick fog of self-criticism. So, yeah, it's definitely causing very clear, tangible problems in every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: At the time this started, did buying the townhouse feel like a major life stressor for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. I mean... people always tell you that buying real estate is stressful, right? But they usually just mean the underwriting process, or the bidding wars, or the appraisal contingencies. They don't really prepare you for the aftermath. Calling it a major life stressor almost feels like an understatement. \n\nI remember sitting at the title company's office on closing day, and the escrow agent slid this stack of documents across the conference table—it had to be at least three inches thick—and every single time I signed my name or initialed the bottom of a page, it literally felt like I was signing away my peace of mind. Because, you know, being twenty-seven and taking on a thirty-year mortgage on a single income... it’s terrifying. You look at the amortization schedule and see all those numbers, the thousands of dollars in interest, and the sheer weight of it just presses down on your chest. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying earlier about the baseboards and the HVAC, the physical reality of owning the place just hit me like a freight train. It felt like I had suddenly taken on a second full-time job where the stakes were my entire financial future, and I felt completely, utterly unqualified for it. I just couldn't turn my brain off. I would literally lie awake at night, staring at the ceiling, running through worst-case scenarios—wondering if the water heater was going to burst while I was sleeping and flood the downstairs flooring, which I'd then have to replace out of pocket, and how that would impact my emergency fund, and whether my homeowner's insurance would fight the claim. \n\nSo, yeah, \"major life stressor\" is definitely the right phrase. It was just this all-consuming pressure that completely took over my life from the exact moment they handed me that envelope of keys.\n\nDoctor: Does owning the townhouse still feel like a major ongoing source of stress for you?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, absolutely. It never stops. Honestly, even when I’m having a decent day—like, maybe I actually managed to sleep for a few hours, or I got a handle on my inbox for five minutes so the fog lifts just a tiny bit—the stress is still just radiating off the walls of that place. Because... well, it’s not just the normal maintenance stuff anymore. It’s this constant, heavy feeling that I am being scrutinized. \n\nI am just absolutely convinced that the mortgage company is monitoring me. Like, they’re watching my accounts, they’re tracking my logins on the payment portal, just waiting for me to slip up by even a fraction of a second. And it’s not just them, it’s the people at work, too. Remember how I mentioned my supervisor checking in on me earlier? That wasn't just her being supportive. I can tell they are actively watching for my mistakes. Every little spreadsheet error, every delayed email about a freight schedule, they're documenting it. \n\nI just know they are all kind of... building this massive case against me to prove that I am completely irresponsible and incompetent. Because the end goal—and I can see all the logistics of how this would play out, because analyzing systems is literally how my brain works—the end goal is to get me fired so I miss a payment, and then they can just swoop in and take the townhouse from me. They want to repossess it. They want to prove I never deserved to own property in the first place because I'm a fraud. \n\nSo I have to be on high alert twenty-four-seven. Every piece of mail with a bank logo on it, every single Slack message from my boss, every time HR walks past my desk... I have to analyze all of it to see if it's part of the trap they're setting. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like living in a pressure cooker that never, ever vents, because even when I try to relax on the couch, I'm just sitting there waiting for them to finally come and take the house away.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two years and three months ago, had you ever gone through another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly depressed or weighed down by guilt?\n\nPatient: Um... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, if I think back to before all of this started... before I got the keys and everything just kind of collapsed in on me... I was actually a really, really put-together person. \n\nLike, when I was finishing my bachelor's degree, or when I first started out in logistics, sure, I had stressful weeks. You know how it is, you have a big project, or quarter-end reporting, or a supply chain bottleneck, and you feel the pressure. You might pull a late night or feel a little overwhelmed for a few days. But it was never... it was never *this*. I never had a time where I felt this completely suffocating guilt, or where I was just so weighed down by my own perceived failures that I couldn't function for weeks at a time. \n\nHonestly, before the townhouse, I was the person everyone else relied on to keep things organized. I was the one making the itineraries for weekend trips, color-coding the shared calendars, keeping everyone on track. I felt capable. I felt like I had a handle on my life and my career. So, no, I never went through anything even remotely like this before. I never just sat around hating myself or feeling like this absolute fraud for weeks on end. It really was just... like a switch flipped the exact second I took on that mortgage. Everything before that was just normal, everyday life.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your mortgage company and coworkers are actually working together to get you fired so you lose the townhouse?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, I do. I know it sounds... I know how it sounds when I say it out loud in a doctor's office, but I am absolutely certain of it. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s a reality that I live with every single second of every single day. \n\nEven when I’m having a decent day—like I said, if I get a little sleep or the fog lifts just enough for me to breathe for a minute—that underlying truth doesn't change. I am still completely, one hundred percent convinced that the mortgage company and the people at work are coordinating this. They are watching every single thing I do. They are just waiting, watching for any tiny mistake, any slip-up, any delayed email or minor error on a spreadsheet, so they can build this massive, undeniable case against me. \n\nAnd the whole point of it, the entire reason they are doing this, is to prove that I am fundamentally irresponsible. They want to prove that I am a fraud, that I am incompetent, and that I never deserved to have that townhouse in the first place. They want to get me fired so I can't make the payments, and then they can just swoop in and take it all away from me. I see the logistics of it so clearly. I know it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I'm definitely not a general worrywart. My sister is kind of like that, actually. She watches the news and gets completely spun out about macroeconomic trends, or she worries about whether her kids are going to catch whatever bug is going around at daycare, or she frets about getting a flat tire on the highway. I really don't do any of that. \n\nMy brain honestly just doesn't have the bandwidth to worry about random external things, because it is entirely consumed by what I am doing wrong. So if you mean worrying about things outside of my control, like the weather or traffic or other people's problems, no, I don't really care about any of that. I only agonize over things where I am the variable that ruins everything. \n\nLike, if a freight shipment is delayed because of a massive storm in the Pacific, I don't worry about the storm itself. The storm is just a data point in my logistics software. But I will absolutely spend twelve straight hours agonizing over the fact that I should have somehow anticipated that storm and rerouted the shipment through a different port, and how the delay is a direct reflection of my personal incompetence, and how I have completely let the entire supply chain team down. \n\nSo it is never a broad, generalized worry about a bunch of different topics. It is extremely specific. It is just a very targeted, relentless focus on my own failures and the ways I am messing up my job or my house. If a situation doesn't involve me making a mistake or being a terrible employee or homeowner, it just doesn't even register on my radar. So, no, I wouldn't say I worry about a lot of different things at all.\n\nDoctor: Has this harsh self-criticism been part of how you’ve been for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Um... most of my adult life? No, definitely not. I mean, back in college and even during my first couple of years working as a junior analyst, I didn't feel this way at all. I was actually pretty confident in my work back then. I knew I was doing a good job, and if I made a minor typo on a supply chain spreadsheet, I just fixed it and moved on. I didn't spend twelve hours agonizing over it and assuming I was going to bankrupt the company. \n\nThis really started... well, I know exactly when it started. It's been going on for about two years and three months. I know the exact timeline because that was right after I bought my townhouse. Which, you know, is supposed to be this huge, exciting milestone, especially being able to do it in my mid-twenties. But almost the second I signed the closing papers and got the keys, this immense pressure just started to build. Because a house isn't just a place to live, it's a massive financial asset, and keeping it up is entirely on my shoulders. \n\nSuddenly I was entirely responsible for the property taxes, the HOA compliance, the roof, the plumbing, the structural integrity... and if any of it went wrong, it would be a catastrophic failure on my part. I started feeling this intense, suffocating pressure to keep everything perfect so the property value wouldn't drop. Every time the HVAC made a weird noise or I saw a dandelion in the front mulch, I felt like I was completely failing at this huge responsibility. And then that feeling just sort of... bled over into my job and everything else. \n\nIt was like a switch flipped exactly two years and three months ago where suddenly every single action I took had these massive, high-stakes consequences, and I was convinced I was ruining all of them. So before I bought the place, no, I wasn't like this. But ever since that closing date, the pressure of keeping the townhouse just compounded and compounded, and that constant, harsh criticizing voice became my entire baseline.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after buying your townhouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it’s been about two years and three months. I know that sounds weirdly specific to keep repeating, but when you buy real estate, the dates are just completely burned into your brain, you know? You have the inspection date, the closing date, the day the first mortgage payment is due... and it was right around then. Right after I actually got the keys and moved my boxes in, that's when it all just started. \n\nBecause suddenly it wasn't just a fun Zillow listing anymore. It was real. The pressure of keeping it up just began to build almost immediately. I remember sitting in the living room on the floor—because my sofa hadn't been delivered yet, there was a whole logistics issue with the freight carrier, which of course I spent hours analyzing because that's what I do for a living—but I was sitting on the floor just looking at the baseboards and realizing that if there was termite damage, or if the foundation settled, or if I didn't maintain the HVAC filters properly, it was one hundred percent my fault. I was solely responsible for protecting this massive financial asset. \n\nAnd that pressure, that absolute crushing weight of knowing I had to keep everything perfect so the property value wouldn't tank... it just started building and building from that exact point two years and three months ago. It just snowballed from the house into my job and into every single thing I do. So, yeah, that was definitely the catalyst.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings caused clear problems in your life, like at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, they definitely have. It's... it's actually become a pretty significant issue across the board, honestly. I mean, starting with my social life, which feels almost nonexistent right now. I've missed so many plans with friends because I just get entirely bogged down in my own head. Like, a friend will text me to meet up for coffee or grab dinner, and instead of just saying yes, I start spiraling about how I don't deserve to go out because I haven't finished organizing the utility closet, or because I'm just going to bring everyone down with my incompetence. So I just isolate myself. I cancel at the last minute, or I just don't reply until it's too late, and then I feel even worse about being a terrible friend. \n\nAnd then at home... well, the townhouse, which is the whole source of this pressure to begin with, is just... it's getting messier than I can stand. Which is so deeply ironic, right? Because I'm so obsessed with keeping it perfect and maintaining the property value, but the actual day-to-day upkeep is just slipping through my fingers. I'll look at a pile of mail on the counter or a dusty baseboard, and instead of just cleaning it, I get completely paralyzed by the fact that I let it get that way in the first place. I just stare at it and mentally berate myself for being such a failure of a homeowner, to the point where I literally can't summon the energy to pick up a sponge. So the mess just compounds, and the guilt compounds with it.\n\nBut the worst part, the part that's really terrifying me, is work. Because logistics and operations, it's all about precision. You have to be on top of a million moving parts. And I've been making mistakes. Not huge, catastrophic ones yet, but enough little slip-ups—missing an email about a freight embargo, or miscalculating a warehouse turnaround time—that my supervisor actually pulled me aside last week. She started checking in on my performance, asking if my workload was too heavy or if I needed additional support. Which, of course, just sent me into an absolute tailspin, because having your boss ask if you're struggling is basically confirmation that you are failing at your job. \n\nI'm still working, I haven't taken a leave of absence or anything, but it takes so much more effort just to keep up with my basic responsibilities. I have to triple-check every single spreadsheet cell because I don't trust my own brain anymore. What used to take me an hour now takes me three, just because I'm constantly second-guessing myself and fighting through this thick fog of self-criticism. So, yeah, it's definitely causing very clear, tangible problems in every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: At the time this started, did buying the townhouse feel like a major life stressor for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. I mean... people always tell you that buying real estate is stressful, right? But they usually just mean the underwriting process, or the bidding wars, or the appraisal contingencies. They don't really prepare you for the aftermath. Calling it a major life stressor almost feels like an understatement. \n\nI remember sitting at the title company's office on closing day, and the escrow agent slid this stack of documents across the conference table—it had to be at least three inches thick—and every single time I signed my name or initialed the bottom of a page, it literally felt like I was signing away my peace of mind. Because, you know, being twenty-seven and taking on a thirty-year mortgage on a single income... it’s terrifying. You look at the amortization schedule and see all those numbers, the thousands of dollars in interest, and the sheer weight of it just presses down on your chest. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying earlier about the baseboards and the HVAC, the physical reality of owning the place just hit me like a freight train. It felt like I had suddenly taken on a second full-time job where the stakes were my entire financial future, and I felt completely, utterly unqualified for it. I just couldn't turn my brain off. I would literally lie awake at night, staring at the ceiling, running through worst-case scenarios—wondering if the water heater was going to burst while I was sleeping and flood the downstairs flooring, which I'd then have to replace out of pocket, and how that would impact my emergency fund, and whether my homeowner's insurance would fight the claim. \n\nSo, yeah, \"major life stressor\" is definitely the right phrase. It was just this all-consuming pressure that completely took over my life from the exact moment they handed me that envelope of keys.\n\nDoctor: Does owning the townhouse still feel like a major ongoing source of stress for you?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, absolutely. It never stops. Honestly, even when I’m having a decent day—like, maybe I actually managed to sleep for a few hours, or I got a handle on my inbox for five minutes so the fog lifts just a tiny bit—the stress is still just radiating off the walls of that place. Because... well, it’s not just the normal maintenance stuff anymore. It’s this constant, heavy feeling that I am being scrutinized. \n\nI am just absolutely convinced that the mortgage company is monitoring me. Like, they’re watching my accounts, they’re tracking my logins on the payment portal, just waiting for me to slip up by even a fraction of a second. And it’s not just them, it’s the people at work, too. Remember how I mentioned my supervisor checking in on me earlier? That wasn't just her being supportive. I can tell they are actively watching for my mistakes. Every little spreadsheet error, every delayed email about a freight schedule, they're documenting it. \n\nI just know they are all kind of... building this massive case against me to prove that I am completely irresponsible and incompetent. Because the end goal—and I can see all the logistics of how this would play out, because analyzing systems is literally how my brain works—the end goal is to get me fired so I miss a payment, and then they can just swoop in and take the townhouse from me. They want to repossess it. They want to prove I never deserved to own property in the first place because I'm a fraud. \n\nSo I have to be on high alert twenty-four-seven. Every piece of mail with a bank logo on it, every single Slack message from my boss, every time HR walks past my desk... I have to analyze all of it to see if it's part of the trap they're setting. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like living in a pressure cooker that never, ever vents, because even when I try to relax on the couch, I'm just sitting there waiting for them to finally come and take the house away.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two years and three months ago, had you ever gone through another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly depressed or weighed down by guilt?\n\nPatient: Um... no. No, I really haven't. I mean, if I think back to before all of this started... before I got the keys and everything just kind of collapsed in on me... I was actually a really, really put-together person. \n\nLike, when I was finishing my bachelor's degree, or when I first started out in logistics, sure, I had stressful weeks. You know how it is, you have a big project, or quarter-end reporting, or a supply chain bottleneck, and you feel the pressure. You might pull a late night or feel a little overwhelmed for a few days. But it was never... it was never *this*. I never had a time where I felt this completely suffocating guilt, or where I was just so weighed down by my own perceived failures that I couldn't function for weeks at a time. \n\nHonestly, before the townhouse, I was the person everyone else relied on to keep things organized. I was the one making the itineraries for weekend trips, color-coding the shared calendars, keeping everyone on track. I felt capable. I felt like I had a handle on my life and my career. So, no, I never went through anything even remotely like this before. I never just sat around hating myself or feeling like this absolute fraud for weeks on end. It really was just... like a switch flipped the exact second I took on that mortgage. Everything before that was just normal, everyday life.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your mortgage company and coworkers are actually working together to get you fired so you lose the townhouse?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, I do. I know it sounds... I know how it sounds when I say it out loud in a doctor's office, but I am absolutely certain of it. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s a reality that I live with every single second of every single day. \n\nEven when I’m having a decent day—like I said, if I get a little sleep or the fog lifts just enough for me to breathe for a minute—that underlying truth doesn't change. I am still completely, one hundred percent convinced that the mortgage company and the people at work are coordinating this. They are watching every single thing I do. They are just waiting, watching for any tiny mistake, any slip-up, any delayed email or minor error on a spreadsheet, so they can build this massive, undeniable case against me. \n\nAnd the whole point of it, the entire reason they are doing this, is to prove that I am fundamentally irresponsible. They want to prove that I am a fraud, that I am incompetent, and that I never deserved to have that townhouse in the first place. They want to get me fired so I can't make the payments, and then they can just swoop in and take it all away from me. I see the logistics of it so clearly. It’s a completely orchestrated plan, and I know for a fact that they are working together to make it happen. I just... I know it."
